<note>The letter "A" appearing in numerical columns likely serves as a symbol for 10 or 12, or as a specific astronomical marker. The letter "G" appears to represent the Golden Number or a degree marker (Gradus). The dominical letters (a-g) repeat every seven days to track days of the week.</note>

<summary>This page contains a complex astronomical table used for computus—the calculation of the calendar. It tracks the solar and lunar cycles (Metonic cycle) across January, March, April, and September, providing data on the length of nights and the timing of celestial events in hours and minutes.</summary>
<keywords>lunar cycle, solar cycle, computus, astronomical table, Metonic cycle, dominical letters, medieval science</keywords>

# Altitude of the sun | Shadow of a man <note>original: "Umbra hois" (Umbra hominis). This refers to the length of a shadow cast by a person of average height, used as a practical sundial.</note>

->8 9 10 before noon<- <note>original: "ant" (ante meridiem)</note>
->4 3 2 after noon<- <note>original: "post" (post meridiem)</note>

<note>The table uses medieval Arabic numerals where 4 resembles a crossed loop, 5 resembles a lowercase 'h', 7 is a caret (Λ), and 9 has a long cursive tail. Red ink (rubrication) is used for headers and the dominical letter 'a', marking Sundays. The columns provide solar altitudes (alti solis) and human shadow lengths (Umbra hois) in feet (pedes) and parts (ptes) for various times of the day (8 AM/4 PM, 9 AM/3 PM, 10 AM/2 PM).</note>

<summary>This page contains a technical table for calculating the sun's altitude and the corresponding length of a human shadow at specific hours of the day. It serves as a medieval scientific tool for timekeeping and solar observation, organized by the days of the month and dominical letters.</summary>
<keywords>solar altitude, shadow length, gnomonics, medieval astronomy, timekeeping, dominical letters, feet and parts</keywords>

# First syzygy, opposition, and mean, with the hours of day and night and the positions of the sun

<note>A "syzygy" refers to the alignment of three celestial bodies, such as the Sun, Earth, and Moon, during a new moon or full moon. "Opposition" specifically refers to the full moon.</note>

| | | First Syzygy | | | Opposition Syzygy | | | Mean Syzygy | | | Equal Hours | | | | | | Quantity | | | | | Quantity | | | | | | | | |

### For the imposthume <note>An "imposthume" is an archaic term for an internal swelling or abscess.</note> <gloss>at</gloss> Take fair May butter <note>Butter made in May was highly prized in medieval medicine for its supposed purity and healing properties.</note> and also take
Henbane <note>A toxic plant in the nightshade family used historically as a sedative and painkiller.</note> and the butter and grind them in a mortar
<margin>at</margin> well together and then <insert>take</insert> it out of the mortar and put
it in a fair pan and set it over the fire and fry
it together a little and then strain it and it
is good for the imposthume //

---

### For a tight chest and for the colic Take honey and
purify it over the fire until it be clean, and take licorice
beaten into powder and mix it <gloss>with</gloss> until it be thick
and when it is almost cold take powder of ginger
and mix it therewith and stir it well together /

<meta>This page continues a series of medical recipes, likely from a 15th-century English leechbook or household manual. The previous page likely contained similar herbal remedies.</meta>

<insert>for an ague <note>a fever with shivering fits, often malaria-like</note> or a hot access <note>a sudden attack of fever</note></insert>

Take houseleek <note>original: "sengre"</note> or barberries or setwall <note>original: "sedewale"; a root similar to ginger or valerian</note>, twenty cloves, and put them into a pottle <note>a liquid measure of about two quarts</note> of clean well water, six ounces. And then give the sick person to drink when his great heat is upon him; and if he must, use this three or four times, and as often as he needs, make more.

---

### To make a plaster for the ague or for fantasy in the head <note>"Fantasy" here refers to delirium, hallucinations, or mental wandering caused by fever</note>

Take sour bread made of wheat—a good portion of the best part—and take the juice of plantain in every jot <note>every drop or bit</note>, and as much of vervain, and as much of woman's milk from one who nourishes a male child <note>original: "knaue child"; a common requirement in medieval medicine based on the belief that milk for a boy had different humoral properties</note>. And while all is cold, work it into a paste and make a plaster that may cover the head; for these medicines are proven for truth.

<summary>This page provides two medieval medical recipes: a herbal drink to break a high fever and a topical plaster made of wheat bread, herbs, and breast milk to treat delirium and head pains.</summary>
<keywords>ague, fever, houseleek, setwall, plantain, vervain, delirium, medieval medicine, herbal remedies</keywords>

# Page 27

<note>This page is dominated by a large, faded illustration known as the "Zodiac Man" or *Homo Signorum*. In the medieval and early modern periods, physicians believed that the macrocosm of the stars directly influenced the microcosm of the human body.</note>

->**The Man of Signs**<-

<note>The central figure is a man standing within a green border. Though the ink has faded and the text from the reverse side bleeds through the parchment, one can still discern the twelve signs of the zodiac mapped onto his anatomy. This diagram served as a medical calendar, instructing practitioners on when—and when not—to perform surgeries or bloodletting based on the position of the moon in the zodiac.</note>

*The celestial correspondences are as follows:*

| Zodiac Sign | Anatomical Region |
| :--- | :--- |
| **Aries** <note>The Ram</note> | The Head, Eyes, and Face |
| **Taurus** <note>The Bull</note> | The Neck and Throat |
| **Gemini** <note>The Twins</note> | The Shoulders, Arms, and Hands |
| **Cancer** <note>The Crab</note> | The Breast, Ribs, and Lungs |
| **Leo** <note>The Lion</note> | The Heart and Stomach |
| **Virgo** <note>The Virgin</note> | The Belly and Intestines |
| **Libra** <note>The Scales</note> | The Kidneys and Hips |
| **Scorpio** <note>The Scorpion</note> | The Secret Parts <note>Genitals and excretory system</note> |
| **Sagittarius** <note>The Archer</note> | The Thighs |
| **Capricorn** <note>The Goat</note> | The Knees |
| **Aquarius** <note>The Water-Bearer</note> | The Legs and Shins |
| **Pisces** <note>The Fish</note> | The Feet |

<note>The illustration is heavily worn, particularly in the center. Faint traces of red and black ink visible around the figure are "ghost" images from the writing on the back of the page.</note>

<summary>This page features a "Zodiac Man" diagram, a traditional medical illustration mapping the twelve astrological signs onto the human body to guide timing for medical treatments. It represents the historical intersection of astrology, astronomy, and medicine.</summary>
<keywords>Zodiac Man, Homo Signorum, medical astrology, bloodletting, anatomy, celestial influence, medieval medicine</keywords>

# Phlebotomy and the Zodiac Man

Aries: beware, there be ware <note>The scribe repeats "beware" for emphasis</note> of cutting <note>Refers to phlebotomy or surgical incision</note>; Gemini: beware.

<margin>
March
April
May
June
July
August
September
October
November
December
</margin>

Taurus: beware of cutting in the neck while the moon is in that sign.

Gemini: beware of cutting in the arm and the shoulder and the veins there; especially beware of cutting in the cephalic vein <note>original: "capital veyne"; the primary vein of the arm used in bloodletting</note> <unclear>the the sons ng of the s-</unclear>

Cancer: beware of cutting in the breast and the ribs for fear of hurting the stomach and lungs, and beware of the heart.

Leo: beware of cutting or hurting of the sinews <note>tendons or nerves</note> and sides and loins and ribs; cut no vein in the back nor out of the sides.

Virgo: cut no wound in the belly <note>original: "wombe"; in this period, "womb" referred generally to the abdomen or stomach for both sexes</note> nor in the lower parts while in this sign.

Libra: beware of cuttings in the belly, in the navel, and in the lower places; nor cut the vein that runs <unclear>that</unclear>

Scorpio: beware of cutting in the reproductive organs <note>original: "gendrynge members"</note> for fear of hurting them with the [surgical] irons while the moon is in this sign.

Sagittarius: beware of cutting in the thighs and sinews; do not cut any wound nor <term>surfeit</term> <note>In this medical context, likely referring to an incision that causes an excess or "over-bleeding"</note> in those places while in this sign.

Capricorn: beware of cutting in the kneecap <note>original: "knee pappe"</note> nor of the veins in these places; neither rise nor do [work there].

Pisces: beware that you do not cut nor hurt the foot with the iron while the moon is in this sign.

---

->Aquarius: beware of cutting in the calves of the legs and veins that go to the calf of the ankles<-

<summary>This page provides a guide to the "Zodiac Man," a medieval medical concept warning practitioners not to perform surgery or bloodletting on specific body parts when the moon is in the corresponding astrological sign. It lists the twelve signs and their anatomical associations, from Aries (head) to Pisces (feet).</summary>
<keywords>Zodiac Man, phlebotomy, bloodletting, astrology, medieval medicine, anatomy, moon signs, surgery</keywords>

Aries has the head and face. Taurus has the neck and throat <unclear>voice during</unclear>.
Gemini has the shoulders, arms, and hands. Cancer has the heart, chest, and
lungs. Leo has the heart, back, sides, and stomach. Virgo has the
liver <note>original: "ep'" for "hepar"</note> and intestines. Libra has the loins, kidneys, and hips. Scorpio has the
seed vessels <note>referring to the reproductive organs</note> and the thighs. Sagittarius has the hips and thighs.
Capricorn has the knees. Aquarius has the shins and legs. Pisces has the feet.

<note>This text describes the "Zodiac Man" (Homo Signorum), a common medieval concept where each astrological sign governs a specific part of the body. Doctors used this to determine when it was safe to perform surgery or bloodletting; for example, one should not bleed the feet when the moon is in Pisces.</note>

<summary>This page contains a mathematical table for calculating the Moon's position in the zodiac and a list of anatomical associations for each sign. It serves as a practical guide for medieval iatromathematics, or medical astrology.</summary>

### A table for knowing which planet rules for any hour of the day or night, beginning:

<margin>
The first hour of the day
</margin>

<margin>
Phlegm <note>original: "Fleuma"</note>
and
choler <note>original: "colera"</note>
comes
</margin>

<margin>
Hot and dry. It is fervent and dry. Cold and moist. Hot and moist.
Choler is dry and prevailing. Phlegm is cold. Blood is hot and moist.
</margin>

| | Sunday | Monday | Tuesday | Wednesday | Thursday | Friday | Saturday |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Sun | Moon | Mars | Mercury | Jupiter | Venus | Saturn |
| 2 | Venus | Saturn | **Sun** | Moon | Mars | Mercury | Jupiter |
| 3 | Mercury | Jupiter | Venus | Saturn | **Sun** | Moon | Mars |
| 4 | Moon | Mars | Mercury | Jupiter | Venus | Saturn | **Sun** |
| 5 | Saturn | **Sun** | Moon | Mars | Mercury | Jupiter | Venus |
| 6 | Jupiter | Venus | Saturn | **Sun** | Moon | Mars | Mercury |
| 7 | Mars | Mercury | Jupiter | Venus | Saturn | **Sun** | Moon |
| 8 | Sun | Moon | Mars | Mercury | Jupiter | Venus | Saturn |
| 9 | Venus | Saturn | **Sun** | Moon | Mars | Mercury | Jupiter |
| 10 | Mercury | Jupiter | Venus | Saturn | **Sun** | Moon | Mars |
| 11 | Moon | Mars | Mercury | Jupiter | Venus | Saturn | **Sun** |
| 12 | Saturn | **Sun** | Moon | Mars | Mercury | Jupiter | Venus |
| 13 | Jupiter | Venus | Saturn | **Sun** | Moon | Mars | Mercury |
| 14 | Mars | Mercury | Jupiter | Venus | Saturn | **Sun** | Moon |

| 22 | **Sun** | Moon | Mars | Mercury | Jupiter | Venus | Saturn |
| 23 | Venus | Saturn | **Sun** | Moon | Mars | Mercury | Jupiter |
| 24 | Mercury | Jupiter | Venus | Saturn | **Sun** | Moon | Mars |

---

Jupiter and Venus are good; Mercury, however, is so when [not] malicious. <note>Mercury was considered "neutral" or "convertible," taking on the good or bad qualities of the planets it was associated with.</note>
The Sun and Mars, along with the Moon, are middling.
Each planet produces a specific <term>humor</term> <note>The four bodily fluids—blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile—believed to control health and temperament.</note>:
Saturn produces melancholy and melancholic sicknesses.
Mars produces choler and choleric sicknesses.
Jupiter, the Sun, and Venus produce blood and its sicknesses.
The Moon produces phlegm and its sicknesses.
Mercury produces all equally, according to how it is joined.
